### The Anarchist Cookbook · `the-anarchist-cookbook` · 5 ban row(s)

**Text:** Refused classification by the Australian Classification Board in 1985 and reaffirmed RC in 2016, banning sale under the National Classification Code. Banned in New Zealand in 1994 by the Indecent Publications Tribunal. A long-standing Canadian customs restriction was lifted in 2002. In the United Kingdom, possession has produced repeated convictions under section 58 of the Terrorism Act 2000 — including Oliver Bel and Ben John (both 2021, three years each) and Claudiu Stefan Cristea (2025) — though earlier 2007 and 2017 prosecutions ended in acquittals.

**fast:** all 12 claims covered

**strong:** all 11 claims covered

```
R1: Australia · Government / national · action: banned · started: 1985 · status: active · stated reasons: Violence · note: "Refused classification by the Australian Classification Board in May 1985 and re-classified RC on 31 October 2016, on the basis that the book "could promote, incite or instruct in matters of crime or violence" — equivalent to a national ban on sale under the National Classification Code Table 1(c)." · sources: Wikipedia (List of books banned by governments); Wikipedia — The Anarchist Cookbook
R2: New Zealand · Government / national · action: banned · started: 1994 · status: active · stated reasons: Violence · note: "Officially banned in New Zealand in 1994 by the Indecent Publications Tribunal as objectionable matter under New Zealand classification law." · sources: FYI.org.nz - New Zealand Official Information; Wikipedia — The Anarchist Cookbook
R3: United Kingdom · Government / national · action: banned · started: 2021 · status: active · actor: UK Crown Courts (Section 58, Terrorism Act 2000) · stated reasons: Violence · note: "Possession of The Anarchist Cookbook has led to multiple UK terrorism prosecutions under section 58 of the Terrorism Act 2000. Convictions include Oliver Bel (2021, 23-year-old Cambridge mathematics graduate, three years' imprisonment), Ben John (2021, three-year sentence: two custody plus one year on licence), and Claudiu Stefan Cristea (2025). Earlier prosecutions — a 17-year-old arrested in 2007 (acquitted October 2008) and Joshua Walker in 2017 (acquitted on academic-purpose grounds) — did not result in conviction." · sources: Wikipedia (List of books banned by governments); Wikipedia — The Anarchist Cookbook
R4: Canada · Customs / border · action: restricted · started: unknown · ended: 2002 · status: rescinded · actor: Canada Customs and Revenue Agency · stated reasons: Drugs / substance use; Political content; Violence · note: "A long-standing customs restriction on importing The Anarchist Cookbook into Canada was lifted in 2002 by the Canada Customs and Revenue Agency, which concluded the book "does not violate either hate or obscenity laws."" · sources: Wikipedia — The Anarchist Cookbook
R5: France · Government / national · action: banned · started: 1971 · status: active · stated reasons: Political content · note: "Banned by French ministerial decree under Article 14 of the Loi n° 49-956 du 16 juillet 1949 sur les publications destinées à la jeunesse. Decree par Raymond Marcellin (gouvernement Jacques Chaban-Delmas - 1969-1972). Citation: JO du 23 juin 1971, p.6051." · sources: Wikipedia — Liste de livres censurés en France (JO du 2 avril 2000, p.5105)
```

### Gender Queer: A Memoir · `gender-queer` · 103 ban row(s)

**Text:** Gender Queer has been widely challenged and removed from schools and libraries in the United States, usually because of sexual illustrations, LGBTQ+ content, and objections to discussions of gender identity.

**fast:** ✗ The book has been widely challenged in the United States. — “Widely challenged” is a generalization; the records do not establish it. · ✗ The book has been removed from schools in the United States. — No U.S. school record states removal; school actions are banned or restricted. · ✗ The book has been removed from libraries in the United States. — No U.S. library record states removal; the library records say restricted or banned. · ✗ The actions were usually because of sexual illustrations. — No stated reason says sexual illustrations; inferred reasons do not count. · ✗ The actions were usually because of LGBTQ+ content. — LGBTQ+ content appears only as an inferred reason in U.S. records; no frequency is established. · ✗ The actions were usually because of objections to discussions of gender identity. — No record states this reason or establishes that it was usual.

**strong:** ✗ Gender Queer has been widely challenged in the United States. — Records do not establish widespread challenges; they record bans and restrictions. · ✗ Gender Queer has been removed from libraries in the United States. — Public-library records show restrictions, not removals. · ✗ The usual reasons were sexual illustrations. — No record states sexual illustrations as a reason or establishes it as usual. · ✗ The usual reasons included LGBTQ+ content. — LGBTQ+ content is stated in R1, but the records do not establish it as a usual reason. · ✗ The usual reasons included objections to discussions of gender identity. — No record states this reason.

### Where the Wild Things Are · `where-the-wild-things-are` · 1 ban row(s)

**Text:** On publication in 1963 a number of children's librarians refused to stock the book on the grounds that its monsters and its depiction of an unrepentantly angry child were too frightening, or would encourage children to defy their parents. Sendak later said the early reviews "were practically all terrible" and that the book was kept off many shelves for its first decade. It has appeared on ALA OIF challenged-books tallies in every decade since.

**fast:** ✗ A number of children's librarians refused to stock the book on publication in 1963. — The record says restricted and challenged in US school and public-library children's sections since publication, not that librarians refused to stock it or that a number did so. · ✗ The refusal was because the child was unrepentantly angry. — The record describes Max's tantrum and unrepentant flight, but does not state that he was an unrepentantly angry child. · ✗ Sendak later said the early reviews were practically all terrible. — No record states this quotation or claim about the reviews. · ✗ The book was kept off many shelves for its first decade. — No record states this outcome or duration. · ✗ The book appeared on ALA OIF challenged-books tallies in every decade since publication. — The record says tallies across multiple decades, not every decade since publication.

**strong:** ✗ On publication in 1963, a number of children's librarians refused to stock the book. — The record does not state that librarians refused to stock it or how many did. · ✗ Sendak later said the early reviews “were practically all terrible.” — No statement by Sendak or quotation about reviews appears in the record. · ✗ The book was kept off many shelves for its first decade. — The record does not establish the number of shelves or a first-decade duration. · ✗ The book has appeared on ALA OIF challenged-books tallies in every decade since publication. — The record says multiple decades, not every decade since publication.

Logged on ALA OIF frequently-challenged tallies across multiple decades." · sources: ALA Office for Intellectual Freedom — Frequently Challenged Books; Wikipedia — List of most commonly challenged books in the United States

**Text:** "La nouvelle Triplice" by Dzelepy was banned in France in 1940 at the national level due to its political content, specifically during the German occupation from 1940 to 1944. The book was included in the 3rd edition of the Liste Otto, which listed undesirable literary works, compiled by the Syndicat des éditeurs under the direction of the German Propaganda-Abteilung. The publisher, Éditions Fustier, faced restrictions as a result of this ban, which remained in effect throughout the occupation.

### Mein Kampf · `mein-kampf` · 8 ban row(s)

**Text:** After World War II, Mein Kampf was banned across much of Europe. In Germany, the state of Bavaria controlled the copyright from 1945 and refused all publication until it expired in 2015, after which a heavily annotated scholarly edition was permitted to prevent misuse. The book is also banned in the Netherlands under hate speech law, listed as extremist material in Russia since 2010, and restricted in Latvia for incitement of racial hatred.

**Prefilter:** name:Europe

**fast:** ✗ After World War II, Mein Kampf was banned across much of Europe. — General regional claim; records do not establish “much of Europe”. · ✗ Bavaria refused all publication until the copyright expired in 2015. — Record says Bavaria blocked reprints of the unabridged work; it does not state “all publication.” · ✗ The edition was permitted to prevent misuse. — No such reason is stated. · ✗ The Netherlands ban is under hate speech law. — No such reason or law is stated. · ✗ The book has been listed as extremist material in Russia since 2010. — Record states it was banned as extremist from 2010, not that it was listed as extremist material. · ✗ The book is restricted in Latvia. — Record states confiscation and charges, not restriction. · ✗ The Latvia restriction was for incitement of racial hatred. — No such stated reason; the record mentions offences under anti-racism law.

**strong:** ✗ After World War II, Mein Kampf was banned across much of Europe. — Records do not establish a ban across much of Europe. · ✗ Bavaria refused all publication until the copyright expired in 2015. — R2 says Bavaria blocked reprints of the unabridged work, not all publication. · ✗ The annotated edition was permitted to prevent misuse. — R2 does not state that purpose. · ✗ Mein Kampf is banned in the Netherlands under hate speech law. — R1 says the ban ended in 2018 and does not cite hate speech law. · ✗ Mein Kampf is restricted in Latvia for incitement of racial hatred. — R7 records confiscation and anti-racism charges in 1995, not a restriction for that stated reason.

### 1984 · `1984` · 27 ban row(s)

**Text:** Nineteen Eighty-Four was banned in the Soviet Union from its publication until 1988, when the first publicly available Russian edition appeared — underground samizdat copies had circulated among dissidents from the mid-1950s. In China the novel was restricted to trusted officials and intellectuals until the late 1980s, reflecting the government's sensitivity to its themes of surveillance and state control. It has also been periodically challenged in American school districts for its political content and sexual references.

### The Satanic Verses · `the-satanic-verses` · 23 ban row(s)

**Text:** Banned in 20 countries between October 1988 and June 1989, beginning with India's import ban on 5 October 1988 and ending with Venezuela in June 1989. Banning governments included Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, Egypt, Bangladesh, Sudan, South Africa (lifted 2002), Sri Lanka, Somalia, Qatar, Malaysia, Indonesia, Singapore, Brunei, Kenya, Tanzania, Thailand, Kuwait and Venezuela. On 14 February 1989 Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Khomeini issued a fatwa calling for Rushdie's death; the fatwa was declared permanent in 2006.

### Lady Chatterley's Lover · `lady-chatterleys-lover` · 16 ban row(s)

**Text:** "Lady Chatterley's Lover" was banned in the United Kingdom in 1928 by the government due to its sexual content. The book faced similar bans in Norway, New Zealand, Australia, Japan, and South Africa throughout the late 1920s to mid-1950s, with Japan's ban resulting in a landmark Supreme Court case in 1957 that upheld the conviction of the translator and editor for obscenity. The official reason for these bans consistently cited explicit sexual content, reflecting the societal norms and legal standards of the time.

**Prefilter:** number:1920

**fast:** ✗ These bans occurred throughout the late 1920s to mid-1950s. — The records give separate start years, not this combined time-span claim. · ✗ The 1957 case upheld the conviction of the translator and editor. — The record says they were convicted by the Supreme Court in 1957, but does not say the case upheld convictions. · ✗ The translator and editor were convicted for obscenity. — The record states they were convicted but does not specify obscenity as the reason for their convictions. · ✗ The official reason for these bans consistently cited explicit sexual content. — The records do not establish a consistent reason across all listed countries; Japan also lists obscenity. · ✗ The bans reflected the societal norms and legal standards of the time. — Commentary not stated in the records.

**strong:** ✗ Japan's ban resulted in a landmark Supreme Court case in 1957. — The record documents a landmark case and 1957 convictions, but does not say the ban resulted in the case. · ✗ The official reason for all the listed bans was explicit sexual content. — The records state sexual content, but not explicit sexual content as the official reason for every ban. · ✗ The bans reflected the societal norms and legal standards of the time. — Commentary not stated in the records.

### Lolita · `lolita` · 22 ban row(s)

**Text:** In December 1956, France followed suit, and the Minister of the Interior banned Lolita; the ban lasted for two years. Reading Lolita in Tehran (2003) is a memoir about teaching government-banned Western literary classics to women in the world of an Islamic Iran, which author Azar Nafisi describes as dominated in the 1980s by fundamentalist "morality squads". Besides the United Kingdom and France, Lolita had been banned in Argentina, Austria, Australia, Belgium, Myanmar, and parts of the United States. Source: Wikipedia

**Prefilter:** number:2003 · number:1980 · name:Minister · name:Interior · name:Tehran · name:Western · name:Islamic · name:Iran · name:Azar · name:Nafisi · name:Austria · name:Belgium · name:Myanmar · date:December 1956

**strong:** ✗ France banned Lolita in December 1956. — R7 gives 1955, not December 1956. · ✗ France followed another ban. — No record states that France followed another ban. · ✗ France’s Minister of the Interior banned Lolita. — R7 names French officials, not the Minister of the Interior. · ✗ The French ban lasted for two years. — R7 says it lasted from 1955 until 1958. · ✗ Reading Lolita in Tehran (2003) is a memoir about teaching government-banned Western literary classics to women in Islamic Iran. — No record mentions this memoir or its subject. · ✗ Azar Nafisi described 1980s Iran as dominated by fundamentalist “morality squads”. — No record mentions Nafisi or this description. · ✗ Lolita was banned in Austria. — No Austria record. · ✗ Lolita was banned in Belgium. — No Belgium record. · ✗ Lolita was banned in Myanmar. — No Myanmar record. · ✗ Wikipedia is the source for the account. — Wikipedia is cited for some records, but not for every claim in the account.

### The Bluest Eye · `the-bluest-eye` · 127 ban row(s)

**Text:** Censors objected to a particularly disturbing sequence in which Pecola Breedlove is raped by her intoxicated father, viewing the graphic depiction of childhood sexual abuse and incest, notably the Soaphead Church episode and the rape scene, as inappropriate for school-aged readers. Offensive language and unsettling psychological trauma depicted in these passages were central to objections raised by parents and administrators.

**Prefilter:** name:Pecola · name:Breedlove · name:Soaphead · name:Church

**strong:** ✗ The book depicts Pecola Breedlove being raped by her intoxicated father. — No record states this plot detail. · ✗ The book depicts childhood sexual abuse and incest, including a Soaphead Church episode and a rape scene. — No record states these content details. · ✗ Censors objected to those passages as inappropriate for school-aged readers. — No record identifies these passages or states this objection. · ✗ Parents objected to offensive language and psychological trauma depicted in those passages. — No record attributes these objections to parents or mentions psychological trauma. · ✗ Administrators objected to offensive language and psychological trauma depicted in those passages. — No record attributes these objections to administrators or mentions psychological trauma.

### The Catcher in the Rye · `the-catcher-in-the-rye` · 5 ban row(s)

**Text:** Profanity and frank adolescent sexuality sparked objections, with repeated use of words such as “goddamn,” “bastard,” and “Chrissakes,” as well as Holden’s musings on sex, rebellion, and disillusionment that censors deemed obscene or immoral. One parent famously counted more than 200 uses of “goddamn” and dozens of other profanities as the basis for concern.

### Ulysses · `ulysses` · 14 ban row(s)

**Text:** Since its publication it has attracted controversy and scrutiny, ranging from an obscenity trial in the United States in 1921 to protracted disputes about the authoritative version of the text. At the trial in 1921 the magazine was declared obscene and, as a result, Ulysses was effectively banned in the United States. In 1919, sections of the novel also appeared in the London literary journal The Egoist, but the novel itself was banned in the United Kingdom until 1936. Source: Wikipedia

**Prefilter:** number:1919 · name:London

### The Adventures of Tintin in the Congo · `tintin-in-the-congo` · 4 ban row(s)

**Text:** Restricted or relocated to adult sections by major UK retailers (Borders, Waterstone's, WHSmith) in 2007 after a Commission for Racial Equality complaint, and moved to locked storage at the Brooklyn Public Library that same year. ### On the Origin of Species · `the-origin-of-species` · 5 ban row(s)

**Text:** On the Origin of Species became a flashpoint in debates over science and religious freedom, with school boards and state governments across the United States seeking to ban or restrict the teaching of evolution from the late 19th century onward. The book's conflict with Biblical creationism reached its defining moment in the 1925 Scopes Trial, where a Tennessee teacher was convicted for teaching evolution — a law struck down only in 1968 by the US Supreme Court.

### Fanny Hill · `fanny-hill` · 8 ban row(s)

**Text:** Fanny Hill is one of the most prosecuted books in English literary history. Shortly after publication in 1749, author John Cleland was arrested for corrupting the King's subjects and the novel was effectively suppressed in Britain; a Massachusetts court declared it obscene in 1821. The book was finally legalised in the United States in 1966, when the Supreme Court ruled in Memoirs v. Massachusetts that it did not meet the constitutional standard for obscenity — a landmark decision in defining the limits of literary censorship.

### A Farewell to Arms · `a-farewell-to-arms` · 2 ban row(s)

**Text:** The novel was banned in Fascist Italy in 1929, where Mussolini's government considered its portrayal of the disastrous retreat at Caporetto an insult to national military honour; it remained prohibited until 1948. In Ireland it was banned in 1953 by the Censorship of Publications Board for sexual content and perceived immorality. In the United States, its magazine serialisation was censored and the book was banned from Boston newsstands for language and sexual content.

### Сущность Сионизма · `sushchnost-sionizma` · 1 ban row(s)

**Text:** In 2019, "Сущность Сионизма" by В. Степин was banned at the national level in Russia, as it was listed on the Federal List of Extremist Materials (entry #4913) by the Ministry of Justice. The ban was upheld by the Хорошевский районный суд города Москвы on March 26, 2019, due to its political content. The book was published by Витязь in 1993 and remains banned as of the latest update in June 2019.
